### 2026-10-13 — The map's choices, and the long arc: normal until it isn't (Michael)

**The map on `M` (for its scope):** (1) it shows what has been seen, by anyone in the world, kept by the world: a new character sees it all, a death wipes nothing (Zomboid may reveal only what you yourself saw; here the world remembers). (2) Player-built walls are shown (they carry `wall.built`; a different ink is allowed). (3) Every player in the world is shown live, with a name; never the dead, never villagers. (4) The world keeps running behind it, alone as with friends: browsing the map is a risk. (5) Minecraft's way: low fidelity, pixel-simplified, and accurate before pretty (when you move, you move on the map). (6) Drawn from the same angle as the screen, as Zomboid's is: map north is the world's north (`-y`, the `n` wall slot), which is up and to the right on screen (`iso.toScreen`), so "map north" means the same to everyone. (7) Pins, notes, a death marker, zoom and pan: someday, wanted.

**The long arc (Michael's picture; two reference images, `docs/images/2026-10-13-vibe-start.webp` and `-vibe-end.webp`):** everything is normal until it is not. A new world starts with no dead anywhere and a whole first day with no danger, villagers talking you through the basics (farming, cooking, healing, weapons). Then the dead come, and the survival game begins (one day the town may be restored while you hold them off; for now you survive). Exploring, or prompted by villagers, you meet spookier things, fight bosses, open new places, and by the end the dead are a nuisance because you have fought something like a demon lord. No story by design (the villagers may tell lore), no end boss, no win screen. What keeps it from being an RPG is the survival half: crops to bring in, supplies running out, weapons spent, so you go home (or later build a forward base). Why go out: the challenge, and things in the far places you badly want at home (Minecraft's End for the Elytra, the Nether for potions): **the more the regions and the survival and crafting systems depend on one another, the better.** The picture people will talk about: a severed demon head brought home and hung in the cosy valley where you started.

**Open, Claude's notes for Michael:** (a) the dead's arrival on day one is a scheduled event, the one place the arc touches the clock law; it is a premise, not a recurring escalation, but it can be made chosen (the dead come with the first nightfall or the first sleep, or a world option skips the quiet day); (b) every region, before it is built, answers what it gives that home wants (the Elytra test); (c) the first picture is brighter and greener than `06`'s dull grass and dusky shade, so the starting register may want revisiting; the shift toward the second is most cheaply palette and light per zone, which the engine already carries; (d) where 1.0 ends (the valley alone, or the valley with one frontier and one boss as proof of the arc) is undecided.

### 2026-10-13 — What this game is: a survival sandbox with a frontier; never harder on a clock; skills off the battlefield; talk that never gates; bosses as gates (Michael)

From a conversation on the game's identity (the Zomboid against Stardew tension since day one), before scoping the map and scripted sequences. **The frame (Claude's proposal, Michael's agreement):** home is Stardew-shaped (the farm, the hearth, the villagers, the reward loop), the frontier is Zomboid-shaped (serious and lethal), and the arc is geography, not story: things get darker the farther and deeper you go, and you choose when to push. The world remembers progress (the cottage, the fields, a cleared place, an opened gate); the character is mortal and takes its skills and what it carries with it. Valheim is the nearest model (a survival game whose regions are gated by bosses, and a great co-op game partly for that reason).

**Never harder on a clock (Michael, a law, in `00`):** no world decay, no shutoffs, no helicopter, no horde night, no population that grows with the days. Every rise in danger is one a player chose ("I went to the bog and accidentally brought some bog danger home with me"), even when danger at home rises with it. The reason: a co-op pair where one plays forty hours a week and the other two; Zomboid and 7 Days to Die punish the one who plays on alone, so he stops playing to wait. The game already passes: needs drain only while a character is in the world (a guest goes dormant after sixty seconds away), crops and fires move only while someone plays, which is a gift to the one who returns, and night cycles without escalating. **To hold when built:** the trickle of `03` refills toward fixed caps answering noise and light, never grows with days survived. **Open (Claude's note, not ruled):** in a shared world one player's choice escalates danger for an absent friend; the cleanest form may be escalation that stays where it was brought (the dead that followed you, a curse riding a character) rather than a world-wide level.

**Skills (Michael):** ~~off the battlefield: no skill makes you hit harder.~~ (superseded 2026-10-19, Skills may touch combat, open for discussion; death takes them, the one real cost of dying: open for discussion, #275) ~~One per station family at most: cooking, farming, healing, smithing, and one for cloth and leather;~~ (superseded 2026-10-19, The valley has no cure; the wizard lives; the witch teaches Potions; gates stop a harvest; every loot table provisional; two old limits struck: fourteen skills, #275) ~~for 1.0, if any, only those that touch non-combat things already in the game~~ (superseded 2026-10-19, Skills may touch combat, open for discussion; death takes them, the one real cost of dying: whether any touches combat is open, #275). Death takes them with the character. Claude's test, agreed in spirit: a skill shows best as things you make (a friend eats your stew, wears your leather), which turns one player's hours into gifts rather than a gap. **Healing** is a skill and a station: several healing items with trade-offs, ~~and the zombie cures made there as soon as the ingredients are gathered, not gated by skill.~~ (superseded 2026-10-19, The cure for the infection is mid to late game, gated by skill: the cure is mid to late game, gated by skill; *Primary's note:* the skill named "healing" here is Herbalism and Potions in the skills scope, #275)

**Recipes: law 1 of `30` restated (Michael):** ~~you know every recipe you need to play effectively from the start~~ (superseded 2026-10-18, The valley first; food heals a little; teachers are safe; the wizard; the dead strike what they break: recipes are learned and earned). ~~Skill may unlock more, but those unlocks are flavour (a fun dish because it is fun), not power; depth and power grow only as far as the rest of the game asks for them (a hard boss could justify stronger food; until such things exist, nothing does). Replaces "all known from the start" as the whole rule.~~ (superseded 2026-10-18, The valley first; food heals a little; teachers are safe; the wizard; the dead strike what they break: recipes are learned and earned, a region's reward among them; whether a learned recipe may be power is open, against the rule that rewards are mostly sideways, #123)

**Talk never gates (Michael):** dialogue trees are in, as exposition and personality, the OSRS register and not forgettable fetch quests; chatting up villagers is how you learn the world. No dialogue is load-bearing and none changes world state (narrowed 2026-10-18, The valley first; food heals a little; teachers are safe; the wizard; the dead strike what they break: a villager who teaches, the wizard among them, gives what he teaches; *Primary's reading:* teaching is its own act at the teacher, a Learn choice, not a line of talk, Michael's to overrule). Talk may read the world and change with it (once the dead arrive in a world that began without them, the townsfolk speak of them), but never write it. Claude's note: this also makes it cheap for multiplayer, a conversation being a panel on the talker's screen with nothing to sync or save. **A scope is owed** (with the map and scripted sequences). Supersedes `04`'s "no quests, no dialogue trees" and the "not an RPG" line of `00` as far as talk goes.

**Bosses gate regions (Michael, as general rules for later):** when the player outgrows the dead, the next region opens behind a deliberately hard boss, the place to prove skill with the weapons and their trade-offs. Two rules with it (Claude's, accepted): the region behind the gate is dangerous for the gear of its time, not only the boss; and a boss can be fought again, so the gate opens once for the world but a friend who missed the fight can still have it. Far off; nothing scheduled.

**Owed before any boss or skills scope:** a session played as the game is meant to be (combat, aggro, the valley, the dead round the spawn; the combat-testing dead are still placed for the proving ground, `TODO.md`), since difficulty has not been felt yet.

### 2026-10-13 — Multiplayer is live: every addition and every change is built for it in the same commit (Michael)

Michael: now that friends play over the relay, every new addition or change must account for multiplayer; it must not lag a week behind and leave a pile of catch-up. **Ruled:** a change that works for the host alone is not done. It runs in the authority's step, a guest reaches it through the same verb, handler, key event, or command as the host's player, its changes travel in the save (the welcome) and the frame, its world state is in the sync check, and it is tried with a guest in two tabs before it is called done. This replaces the co-op tiebreaker of 2026-10-12 ("all else equal, the multiplayer-friendly fork; single player first"), which only broke ties. Written in four places so no session misses it: `CLAUDE.md` (start-here item 6 and the first of the rules, with the checklist), `TODO.md` (above the Status block), `docs/31-multiplayer/08` (now the rules every change follows, the old six habits kept as the floor), and `05-someday.md`'s co-op entry (no longer someday).

### 2026-10-13 — Sleep in a world with friends: time skips only when everyone is in bed (Michael)

**The ruling (Michael):** in a multiplayer world sleep is possible and optional, and both players must be in bed asleep before the time jump starts. **Before:** any one player lying down set the whole world's time scale to 60 (`startSleep`), so a friend still awake had their game fast-forwarded under them, and `wake` set it back to 1 even if another was still asleep. **Now:** the time scale is the world's, computed from who is asleep (`game.timeScaleNow`): the sleep speed only while every living player in the world is asleep, ordinary time otherwise. A dormant player is out of the world's list and a dead one is skipped, so neither blocks nor forces a skip. It is evaluated each step in `game.step` (a guest who joins, leaves, wakes, or dies puts the speed right) and at the moment of lying down and waking (`player.js` takes the rule from `game.js` by `setSleepScaleRule`, so a lone player still gets the skip at once, as `check("sleep")` expects). A sleeper alone in bed while a friend is awake sleeps at ordinary speed, rested and regenerating at the sleeping rate, and is told "Time will pass faster once everyone in the world is asleep." (a lone player or the last one in bed still hears "The night goes on without you."). A sleeper is still never woken by anyone else's doing: they get up when rested and it is morning, and then the world slows for any still asleep. **Verified:** `check("sleep")` 9 of 9, three new rows with two players on one bed (one asleep and one awake: scale 1; both: 60; one wakes: back to 1); the other lone-world checks unchanged (determinism `d9a6eb31`, players 9, save 7, craft 9, wear 4, growth 9, water 8, sway 8, worlds 10), headless the same. **For the multiplayer branch:** the frame rate to a guest must be paced by real time, not by steps, because while the world is at 60 the host runs up to 120 steps per browser frame (the plan for item 1 of the bandwidth work).

### 2026-10-13 — The coder agent's review of docs/31/09, and four rulings for its second step

The coder agent read `09` and asked for changes; Michael relayed them after the first step was built, and ruled on the four that were his. **Adopted:** the owners map stays in the save's wrapper, never in what a guest receives (it already did), and holds a hash of each browser id rather than the id; the host's own character is never offered to a guest, and a newer join with the same browser id replaces the older connection (a refresh with the old socket still open); a protocol number and the save's version on the join instead of a build version, bumped by hand, the refusal naming whose is newer; Rejoin polls whether the world is open and joins once, the same poll feeding the green dot; the host clamps a guest's identity (the name's length, listed hair styles, hex colours); the placeholder creation step behind a flag; a confirm when a host leaves, since its guests are dropped; the obsolete `identityChosen` setting gone; `&as=<name>` to pick an identity on a scripted join. **Declined:** creating a new world as create-then-restore so one path is always exercised: the round-trip check already proves a restored world hashes as one never saved, and every creation would pay a serialise and a restore for nothing.

**Ruled (Michael).** (1) **An offline guest's character goes dormant, Zomboid's way:** after a grace of no more than sixty seconds it leaves the simulation, not drawn and not hunted, its spot and bag kept in the save's wrapper, and it returns there on rejoin; a host leaving makes every guest's character dormant at once before the save. (2) **The store moves to IndexedDB now:** "I do not want to build something now that breaks as soon as we double the size of a map"; the index stays small in localStorage for the screen, the records go to the large store, a save that fails says so on screen. (3) **Longer codes:** two tree words and four letters, with a rate limit on the relay. (4) **Black behind the title** until the art comes; Michael works on the painting; the doc marks it as coming.

### 2026-10-13 — Worlds, identities, characters: the four questions answered, and five more rulings (docs/31/09)

Michael's answers: a world's name typed with a default offered; a character's look fixed after creation ("sprint into the dead" is the way to a new one); a dead character's bookmark on a friend's world kept, Rejoin leading to New character; spectating later with tier 4. His additions, now in `09`: **character creation is a multi-step process**, step 1 the identity, step 2 a placeholder page for the skills and profession to come, with Back and Next as Zomboid has them, so it is plain the identity is one part of a character; **an identity is a stamp**: edited and saved, it applies to every character made from it afterwards and never backfills one already stamped; ~~**hosting is a switch**, "Open to friends" on or off per world, the code drawn and saved the first time it goes on, no separate kind of save~~ (superseded 2026-10-17: Play alone or play with friends; telemetry from every world, #143); **friends' worlds show whether they are open right now**, a green dot the relay answers to a plain request for the code, so the confirmation comes before Rejoin is pressed; and yes, many single-player worlds at different hours, revisited freely, which is the My worlds list.

### 2026-10-13 — Ruled: worlds, identities, characters (docs/31/09), Zomboid's model, before the rest of tier 3

**What Michael found** after his first real join: it is never clear which game you are in. A world is created at load whatever you do, so New game only closes the menu; a hard refresh leaves you unsure whether you are in a new game, your old one, or need to rejoin; a rejoin gives a new survivor; hosting from the title hosts the background world and not the save; the one identity per browser is used in every game, so a name changed for one game is changed for the next; nothing says which saves exist or which were ever hosted.

**Ruling (Michael, in Zomboid's terms, which he asked for by name).** Stop conflating the character creation screen with the character. **An identity** is a name and a look, kept in the browser and reused; **a character** is a survivor in one world, owning its bag, its wounds, and later its skills and story, and **it belongs to that world, full stop**: never transported, gone with the world. Entering any world, your own or a friend's, means making a character for it from an identity; rejoining a world returns you to the character you made there, with no identity asked; a death means a new character in that same world, from any identity. The reason beyond clarity: the moment professions, skills, or story beats attach to a character, the code wants this shape, so it is built now rather than refactored later.

**With it, mine, from the walk-through of a refreshed guest** (asked and answered in the same conversation): no world runs until one is chosen on a Worlds screen that lists this browser's worlds and the friends' worlds it has joined, each with what it is and a Play, ~~Host,~~ Delete, or Rejoin (superseded 2026-10-17: Play alone or play with friends; telemetry from every world, #143); a world keeps its code; the host saves on every join and leave; a browser id on the join is how a world knows whose character is whose; the join carries the build's version; Rejoin keeps trying for a minute while the host is not yet up. The design, the screens, the data, the wire, and the order are `31/09`; its four questions are open (a world's name typed with a default offered; a character's look fixed after creation; a dead character's bookmark kept; spectating later), each with a recommendation. **Built before tier 3's remainder,** since it is what makes playing with a friend legible; the owed "change of name or look on a guest after join" is withdrawn, not a thing by design.

### 2026-10-13 — Ruled: the relay server is the transport; the GitHub Pages site is the client friends open

**What Michael found.** He hosted from the GitHub Pages site in Firefox and joined from Chrome. The host opened its world; the guest landed in a frozen world with no character screen, the dev name, and the default look. The wire today is a `BroadcastChannel`, which the browser delivers only between tabs of one origin in one browser profile, so the join reached nobody: the guest sat on the world it booted locally (a guest skips the title because its world is meant to arrive from the host) and its player, which only the host ever steps, stood still. Expected under the plan (the loopback is tier 1's transport; `31/04` names the relay for the wire), but the Host screen's code looked shareable and its small print promised less than it should have said plainly. He also had to copy the code out of the DOM: the pixel font makes some letters read alike.

**Ruling (Michael).** *The GitHub Pages site is how he tests with people online*, because a URL is far less to hand a friend than an address to paste; so the client stays static and **the transport is the relay server** of `31/04`: a small Node WebSocket process the host and every guest connect to, which forwards the host's frames to the guests and the guests' inputs and commands to the host, and which is the lobby (the host gets a code, guests type it). The same program is tier 4's dedicated server in another mode. Not chosen: WebRTC with a copy-paste handshake (the code becomes a blob of hundreds of characters each way, and some home routers need a relay anyway), WebRTC with a signalling server (two things to run instead of one).

**What follows.** (1) Three small fixes before the relay, one move: the code copyable on the Host screen (a selectable monospace field and a Copy button); a guest that gets no answer within a few seconds told so plainly with the way back to the title, never a frozen world; a guest with no saved identity sent through the character screen before it joins. (2) The relay pulled forward from tier 4, before the rest of tier 3: `tools/relay.js` (or `server/`), no npm dependency unless the hand-written handshake proves worse than `ws`; `net.js` gains a WebSocket transport beside the BroadcastChannel one, the messages unchanged (the join a load, a frame per step, inputs and commands). The Pages site is `https`, so the browser will only open `wss://`: the relay needs TLS, which a free hosting tier gives for nothing and Michael's own machine would need a tunnel for; where it runs is `31/07` question 20. The rest of tier 3 then lands over the relay, and the first real latency numbers answer the lag question of the first session with a measurement rather than a forecast. (3) `31/06` reordered accordingly; TODO's multiplayer line points here.

### 2026-10-13 — The twelfth move: a guest's panel as commands (docs/31/05 item 9, first form)

(the build's record is `docs/history/02-build-log.md`, same heading)

**Ruling.** A guest's inventory panel is a view on its replica, and every change it asks for is a command to the host: a drag into a slot, a drag into a grid (its own bag, the open container, or between them), a drop on the floor, a shift-click across, Rotate, Tidy, a double-click, and every entry of the item and slot menus but Use (which only begins the Use mode, whose apply is the next move's command). The command names things the way the save does: the entry by its uid, the slot by its id, the bag by an address (the player's own, a prop by id with "output" for a station's bag, a floor bag by its entry's uid on its tile, a body by id) — the same shape `containerOpened` already sends a guest. The host runs the command as the guest's player through the very handlers its own panel uses (`ui/inventory-actions.js` `performItemCommand` round `createInventoryActions`), so the rules of what fits where, what gives way, and what is refused are one code path, and a message said on the way reaches the guest through the acting player. A command on a stale view finds nothing by its uid and does nothing; a container not the player's must be within the panel's own closing distance. The guest's menus are built locally from the replica (the labels and the greyed entries are the same), with each entry's action replaced by the send.

### 2026-10-13 — A swing has a wrist: the blade stays level as the arm drops (Michael's reading)

Michael, on the high-to-fall slash: at the end the tip dips, and he suspected the hold rather than the arm. Right: in a swing a held thing points along the forearm, so at the follow-through, with the arm at 35 degrees, the blade pointed at the ground with it. **A pose now has a `wrist`**, degrees the held thing is cocked up from the forearm toward "up" on whichever side it hangs, read from the style's frame (`data/poses.json`, `wrist` per frame; the rig's default is none, so the bow and the throw are unchanged). The slash cocks 10 at the hit, 45 at the follow-through, 30 at the settle, so from the side the blade finishes level and from the front it crosses low rather than pointing down. The sheets check re-baselined on the player-variant sheets.

### 2026-10-13 — The one-handed swing is a high-to-fall slash, never behind the back (Michael)

Michael on the first swing pass, in the frames editor: the behind-the-back part was the problem; a general one-handed swing raises to head level or a smidge higher and no more, never behind the back, and reads as a slash with a sword while still bopping plausibly with a stick; the high-to-fall slash is the play. **Ruled, and in the data:** `one_handed` is raise (pitch 150, the hand beside the head, the body back half a unit), hit on the way down (90, the body dropping a pixel and leaning in), the follow-through low and across the body (35, out 5 across), settle (55). No negative pitch in the style. **The walk's first pass is accepted** ("looks great").

### 2026-10-13 — On the wire the world never pauses (Michael: "never pausing is right and simplest. It's what Zomboid does"; `31/03`, `31/07` question 6 answered)

The first piece of tier 2 of the multiplayer plan, and Michael's question ("has it been accounted for that the single player hitting escape pauses the game and that can't be the case for multi?"). The plan had the rule; the code paused the loop on any screen, so a host's menu would have frozen every guest and a guest's menu would have stopped its own inputs while the host held its last one, a guest walking on through its own pause screen. **Ruled:** a screen pauses the world only in a game played alone. **On the wire, as a host or a guest, the world never pauses:** the title, the menu, the character screen, Settings, and the dead screen are this client's own over a running world; while one is up this client's player stands still (an idle snapshot goes to the world, whatever the keys say) and nothing pressed reaches the game or this client's modes; the pause screen reads "Menu: the world goes on while you are here; your character stands still." A hosting tab's world runs on its title too, so it answers joins there. **Hit-stop is refused on the wire** (`loop.setFreezeAllowed`): no one's blow holds the world for everyone; the camera's kick stays (`31/05` item 13 in its first form; the local hold of the renderer's own clock is polish owed). **Verified in two tabs:** the guest walked east on a held key, opened its menu mid-stride, and stood still through thirty steps with the key still held while frames went on arriving; the host opened its menu, the loop stayed unpaused, thirty frames went out, and the guest's player stood where it had stopped; a freeze asked for on the host was refused. A plain boot keeps its pause and its hash.

### 2026-10-13 — Step 3 of `30` built: the stations, recipes, durability, the tools; the rulings the build forced

**Any lit fire that cooks is a cooking station** (the hearth, the campfire, the inn's hearths): the spec's pot on the hearth is not asked for until the pot is an item worth placing; a row then adds it to the fire's definition. **Ingredients come from everything within reach**: the hands, the belt, your bag, the station's own output, and every container within 2.6 tiles that has been searched (a container's bag exists once it is opened; an unsearched chest holds nothing yet, which is the loot rule, not a crafting one). **The station keeps working while you walk off and while you sleep**: its queue runs in the world's time, so a bed beside the oven bakes the bread by morning; the fire's going out pauses it, never spoils it. **A prop recipe lands beside the station** on the nearest free tile, never in your hands; a chest costs four planks, a workbench five and a log, so the second workbench is yours to carry home. **A worn-out weapon leaves the hand with a word**, nothing dropped, as `30` says; **the finisher's extra damage means a stick may see a sixth off**, which the check allows (the number is a design number, the wear is by hits). **Tools are weapons of five kills** and swing badly; their verbs come with the steps that need them (till with the farms, mine with the quarry), so this step only gives them bodies, recipes, and the anvil's hammer rule (the knife, the hatchet, the pickaxe, the scythe, the spear, the mace, the sword ask for a hammer in hand; the hammer itself and the iron bar do not, so the loop can start). **The habit forks, taken the habit's way:** the recipe queue lives on the prop with the maker's id, not in the panel; Make calls `verbs.run("craft")` by the station's id and nothing else; a Use row names its verb by `action` (the label stays `verb`), so a second player's click resolves through the same table. **Open:** the loom and the dye vats wait on cloth and wool as items (step 7 or 8); the recipe numbers are first guesses, Michael's to tune in `data/recipes.json`.

### 2026-10-13 — Step 2 of `30` built: the needs, under `31/08`'s habits; the rulings the build forced

**The multiplayer plan's six habits are followed from this step on** (`docs/31-multiplayer/08`, another session's branch until it lands; Michael made this session the authoritative build agent): sleep is a field on the world the loop reads (`world.timeScale`), not a dt multiplier or a clock jump; the sleep verb is a row in a new verb table addressed by the bed's id; every prop has an id and every item entry a uid from the world's counter; a fire's feed and burn-out are one mutator each that touch the tile and emit. **Where the habit forked against the simpler way, the habit won:** the sleep verb could have been a closure on the bed's action and the fast-forward a multiplier in the loop; both are the other way, so a command queue can call the same door later. **The sleeper stands by the bed:** the rig has no lying pose; one comes with the art pass. **Waking:** rested and morning (06:00 to 22:00), whichever is later; a bed taken away wakes you. **The forge burns charcoal**, an item added now (the burners of step 6 supply it; until then the dev chest). **Survival is on by default** (hunger, thirst, tiredness drain; the setting still turns it off). **The rates are to the day:** hunger a day and a half, thirst a day, tiredness a day and a quarter, as `30` set; to feel out in play.

### 2026-10-13 — Reading never grows a chunk: the world is the simulation's alone (tier 0 of `31`, item 8 of `31/05`)

(the build's record is `docs/history/02-build-log.md`, same heading)

The first move of the multiplayer plan (`31-multiplayer/`), made on its own branch and small enough to merge under the big pass. Until tonight `map.tileAt` grew a missing chunk on any read, and the renderer, the ground bake, the light, sight, the arrow's scan, and the placement previews all read: so which chunks existed, in what order the wild's dead were made, and where the one generator stood depended on the window's size and the zoom, and a world run with no window (a server) would have diverged from a browser's at its first wild chunk. **Ruled by the build, Michael to overrule:** `tileAt` is for reading and never grows; beyond the grown ring it returns **the blank tile** (plain floor, no walls, no prop, outdoors, frozen so a write to it throws). Growth is **`map.ensureAround`'s ring round each player, four chunks** (was three in play, four at boot; the ground now needs a viewed chunk's neighbours grown, below), and **the mutators'** (`growTile`: `dropItem`, `placeProp`, `setFloor`, `buildWall`, `destroyWall`, `addBracket`). **The ground bakes a chunk only once it and its eight neighbours are grown** (a bake reads `REACH_MAX` tiles into them), draws nothing for one that is not, and never caches a picture of blank land; in play the ring is wider than any view by a chunk, so nothing in view is ever without its picture. Boot grows the spawn's ring before anything reads. The law in `CLAUDE.md`.

### 2026-10-12 — Step 1 of `30` built: the village, in labelled boxes; the rulings the build forced

**A box says what it is:** every furniture or station definition without art is a coloured box with its name drawn on it (`placeholder.label`), as Michael asked; the first tile of a long box carries the name once. **A cottage's byre is behind a rail, not a wall:** the long house's low partition is `wall_fence`, which bounds no room, so the byre and the hearth share one room and its light, which is what a long house is. **Openings can be authored in a state:** the inn's windows boarded from inside (`boards: { in: 3 }`), a cottage's door kicked in (`broken`), the smithy's open front (`open`). **Containers without their own loot open with the chest's table** until step 3 writes theirs. **The plan's coordinates are 29/04's** (two tiles a character from (56, 84)); where its block letters could not hold the room plans it describes, the building was drawn smaller inside the block rather than the block moved. ### 2026-10-12 — Walking through a field makes it wobble (Michael)

"Some but not all of those interactable objects can be pathed through, crops are the big one": any character, the player, the dead, or a villager, walking through an interactable that can be walked entirely through sets it swaying as a click would, which is what makes a field read as a field: the movement interacts with it. Trees and rocks are excluded by the same test, `solid` on the prop (`interact.brushThrough`, called for everyone each step). One round finishes before the next starts (the sway's own 560 ms is the gap), so the sway is never restarted mid-way; walking on keeps it going, standing still in it does not. A definition's `"sway": false` or `clickAnim` holds here too.

### 2026-10-12 — The wobble written in stone, and the bug that had switched it off; the container its own panel; capitals in the headers (Michael)

**The rule, in stone:** anything interactable wobbles when clicked, by default; a sprite definition turns it off with `"sway": false`, and one with a `clickAnim` plays that instead (the well's bucket). **The bug:** nothing had wobbled since step 2 of `23` moved the poke's stamp to the sim clock (`interact.pokeAt`, sim milliseconds) while the renderer still subtracted it from the wall clock (`render/props.js`, `swayOf` and `clickFrame`), so every sway was over before it began; the renderer now reads the sim clock, the one the stamp is in. The frame check's frozen wall clock did not catch it because no check pokes anything; a check for the sway is owed. **Layering:** the bag, the character screen, the belt, and the build list sit above the speech bubbles and the bars over the dead (`z-index` 7 over 6), the menu above them (9), the screens above all (10). **Art in the bag** keeps the whole cell: the cutout edge is painted as inset shadows, not a border, and a stack of art stays centred (the throwing knife and axe sat low and clipped). **An open container is its own panel**, dragged and remembered apart from the bag (`panelPos.container`); its home is to the left of the bag; its X closes it alone. **Panel titles are capitals** (`.inv-title`), to see whether that feels overbearing: the pixel font's lowercase vowels were hard to read at the header's size.

The timber buttons stay everywhere, since they are not stone. This overrules `23`'s "every panel on the kit". **The look:** Minecraft's reads as stone more than the speckled grey did; the frame is now a flat band round a field of tiled brick (his third reference), and every slot and grid cell is a darker cutout with the inset edge, dark top and left and light bottom and right, "sitting waiting for an icon to fill them up". **The font (Q4c): Pixelify Sans**, "the real deal"; VT323 was weighed (a terminal face, monospaced: superb for numbers, typewritten in sentences) and Pixelify preferred. The file comes in under its Open Font License at `data/fonts/PixelifySans.ttf`; `style.css` names it with Segoe as the fallback. Q4a (grey or warmer) stands open; the colours are three fields in `data/sprites/ui_kit.json`.

Michael: Tab opens and closes the bag, C the character screen, each on its own; closing one never closes the other (Escape still closes whatever is open, and carrying furniture closes both; Tab with no bag worn still opens the character screen with the message, `11` check 6). ~~Both are "substantially larger on larger screens", the character screen most, so item art can sit in its slots: the bag's cell is `view.panelCell` (38 px times a half step from 1 to 3, the largest at which a character screen eight cells tall fits nine tenths of the window's height and the character screen beside a rucksack fits its width: 57 px on a 1080p window at the automatic interface size), and a character slot is two cells square.~~ (superseded 2026-10-12: The stone on three things only: the growth with the window is withdrawn, `view.panelCell` returns 38, a character slot is one cell in its cutout edge) **Every item that can be held or worn owes a portrait** (`def.portrait`, a square picture 38 px a side drawn from the same design as its world picture and its bag icon, `26`): a slot shows it at a whole scale; until it comes, a square bag icon stands in, and anything else shows the coloured block with its label. This is definitely the cleanest version of the base model we've ever had. I'd say we have a body! And it probably won't change again until/unless I hire a real human artist." **Ruled:** the body is the rig as it stands tonight (`src/figure/rig.js` and the rules in `draw.js` and `raster.js`, logged below in order); it is not touched again without a ruling here. ### 2026-10-12 — The body stays the rig's; the design agent draws what goes on it (Michael), and three fixes to the rig

Michael tried to have the design agent redraw the base body and found the rig's body "actually nearly perfect": the head's shape kept, the arms right in a way the agent could not reproduce (one capsule hanging from a shoulder joint inside the torso, the near arm in front and the far arm behind by the depth sort, smooth in every facing because it is projected, never drawn). **Ruled:** the body is the rig's numbers and rules (`src/figure/rig.js`, `draw.js`); the design agent is asked for what goes on it (garments, hair, hats, beards, held things), painted as parts on the template, never for the body. **Three changes to the rig, Michael's, 2026-10-12:** (1) **the hip two pixels higher** (`BODY.hip` 15 to 17): the body a smidge smaller, the legs a smidge longer; (2) **a far arm shows a real sliver of skin or nothing** (`FAR_ARM_MIN` 8 fill pixels in `draw.js`; `Frame.cullPart`): the one or two pixels that peeked beside the hip in the side and three-quarter facings, outline against outline, are gone; (3) **no double outline**: in any row where an arm's outline runs straight against another part's outline with no fill between, the nearer part's outline stays and the other pixel becomes what lies beyond it, fill or nothing (`Frame.mergeOutlines`; Michael: "if there isn't a column of skin between two borders, don't draw both"). To make the rules possible the outline pixels now carry the part that drew them; a painted layer still never overwrites an outline. The feet stay as they are (Michael is split on drawn feet; a `feet` layer can change them later). **A console fix on the way:** `__game.setLook({})` now forces a rebake, so an edit to `figure.BODY` shows at once (no key ever included the rig's numbers, so it never did before). **Michael's second look, the same night, three more and then "good enough to stop touching for a good long while":** a far arm's shoulder half is judged apart from its hand's and shows only when most of it does (`FAR_ARM_MIN_UPPER` 24 against the hand's 8), so no strip or shoulder flare along the torso in the three-quarter views while the wrist and hand still peek out at the hip; the torso's front-to-back thickness is the arm's (`torsoD` 3, was 4), so from the side the neck is as wide as the arm, not a block wider than it; the hips are a hair thicker than the legs (`hipD` 3.4, was 3), so the legs' tops never jut a pixel past the torso in the side views. **His play, the same night, found two regressions of the far-arm rule and fixed its shape for good:** a holding arm lost its upper arm (the strip along the torso was under the count), and walking toward or away the back-swung arm lost its hand (a short whole arm has few pixels too). **The rule is now about width, not count:** what is left of a hidden arm goes only if no row of it is wider than two pixels (`FAR_ARM_SLIVER`), judged per half so a hand stays when the shoulder is a sliver; and **a raised arm is never judged** (a pitch over `RAISED_ARM` 40 degrees: holding, swinging, the dead's reach). A frame-by-frame bake of the walk and of the hold at every facing is how it was checked.

### 2026-10-12 — Step 2 of `23` done; what it deferred, and the two checks that prove a move

(the build's record is `docs/history/02-build-log.md`, same heading)

**Deferred by the spec's own defaults, not dropped:** the `actions/` and `map/` splits (D2, D4: not in step 3's way), the pathfinder's allocation (B8: never the biggest spike), and renaming what `main.js` now is (boot's stages, the state, the step order, `render()`) to `game.js`. **A ruling the moves forced:** a split of a drawing module is accepted only when `__game.check("sheets")` (every sheet's pixels) and `check("frame")` (a rendered frame, the wall clock frozen) hash the same before and after; the renderer's animations run on the wall clock by design, which is why the frame check freezes it. Nothing in the sim reads the wall clock; the renderer may.

### 2026-10-12 — Far wild chunks are dropped and their dead sleep with them; the fallen leave the thinking list (step 2 of `23`, group B)

**Ruled by the build, Michael to overrule:** a wild chunk that play has not changed (no floor set, no prop placed or felled, no item dropped or taken, no wall) and that lies more than **eight chunks** from the player is dropped from memory, unless a villager stands in it; the wild is deterministic by hash, so it grows back the same chunk, and **the characters that stood in it sleep on its record and wake when it grows back**, so nothing is lost and nothing is rolled twice (verified: living plus sleeping 116 before and after a 400-tile round trip). This is **a first slice of `03`'s records**, not the milestone: no hopping, no alarm, no lure, no trickle. **A corpse leaves the dead's list for a corpses' list**: drawn, looted, and burning, never iterated by the AI, separation, speech, or the bars. **The light cache is invalidated per chunk** (a change touches its chunk and the eight around it, the reach of any source under a chunk), and **rooms are refound locally** on a wall built or taken down (the two tiles the edge separates name the rooms to refill; outdoors is one region a refill may join). The per-step cost of the village did not change with these; what changed is that it no longer grows with the distance walked.

### 2026-10-12 — The simulation gets one generator and one clock (step 2 of `23`, group C; the co-op law applied)

(the build's record is `docs/history/02-build-log.md`, same heading)

Michael asked that the engine plans be checked against the multiplayer tiebreaker (`CLAUDE.md`; his interest is general, not pressing). The check is written into `23`, step 2, and this is the first fork it decided: **the simulation rolls only through `src/rng.js`**, one seeded generator advanced in step order (the map file's `seed`, default 1), rather than a per-call list of exempt `Math.random`s; **the sim reads time only from `loop.simTime()`** (game seconds stepped), and the bow's "a draw held through a pause is dropped" rule counts the loop's pauses instead of a wall-clock gap; **the rules the sim reads from the settings are a snapshot** (`src/rules.js`), refreshed once a step, so a host could hand a guest the same object and a test in the pane no longer changes the sim by poking storage. Exempt by design: `fire.js`'s embers and smoke (a visual), the wild's `hash2` (position-keyed is better than sequence-keyed there), the renderer's animation clocks.

### 2026-10-12 — The loading shell built (step 1 of `23`), and what the first measured boots ruled

Built and measured the same night (`23`, step 1, "As built"). **Rulings the numbers forced:** the dev server speaks **HTTP/1.1 with keep-alive** (two hundred files took 18 s over one connection per file; 250 ms after); **the atlas is packed tallest first** at boot (registration order filled it to 94 percent, sorted 67), and **the figure sheets are the atlas's budget**: eighteen figure-sized sheets are the bulk of it, so a second atlas page is scheduled for the day the wardrobes grow (`25`) rather than now; the ground kit's sheets and the `zombie` definition's generated body never enter the atlas (never drawn); **boot never waits on an animation frame alone** (a hidden tab gets none; a 40 ms timer stands in). **Kept as specced:** the loading screen is not skippable and shows its stages; everything in the manifest loads and every outfit bakes before the title; the manifest's stamps cache art for a year while source and the manifest stay `no-store`; saves are refused from another origin.

### 2026-10-12 — The engine upgrade is the next task, the docs first (Michael)

Michael, after the whole-codebase review (`23a-review.md`): the game is "already insanely detailed for the type of games I usually make", he intends to fully finish it, and "now is probably a good time to stop and make big engine changes before the game gets more complicated." **Ruled:** the engine upgrade (`23-engine-upgrade.md`, rewritten tonight) is the next task, ahead of the combat polish, the big pass (`22`), and the throwables (`24`). **The order:** 0 the docs made true, so code and docs agree before an implementer reads them; 1 the loading shell; 2 the engine hardened in the review's order (robustness, determinism, growth, ownership); 3 the sprite system, with the slicer the characters need pulled out and first; 4 the UI kit. **The review's verdict is on record so the goal is aimed right:** the frame budget is not the problem (0.74 ms a step and render at the widest zoom with 67 dead); the problems are an exception that stops the game, things that grow without bound, the clock and `Math.random` in the simulation (26 lines, against the co-op rule), and `main.js` owning too much. **Defaults the spec sets, Michael's to overrule:** small verified moves, never a big bang; a target module tree proposed; `figure/` and `render/` split in this pass, `actions/` and `map/` only if in the way; the parent's entity numbers move into `data/tuning.json` unchanged (question 19's ruling still waits); the loading screen is not skippable and shows its stages; the editor edits pixels and definitions, not animation; live reload and hard refresh both. **The sweep's two questions, answered by Michael the same night:** infection's 1.5 days *is* "about two" for now, so the data and the docs agree as they stand, and the infection does nothing yet, so there is time to pivot; **furniture is solid** (question 11 closes): a bed, a table, a chair, and their like block, with exceptions possible later for certain smaller things. The look's questions (Q4a, the palette; Q4c, the font) are deferred to step 4, when the UI is reached. The co-op tiebreaker decided one fork tonight: the simulation gets one seeded generator and a sim clock rather than a per-call exemption list. **Step 0's data-style ruling:** nothing in `data/` is reformatted (the diffs would bury Michael's history in the files he edits); new files use two-space JSON with compact one-line leaf records, the style of `combat.json` and `poses.json`.

Two rulings on the character brief. **No undies, ever, by default:** the goobers are asexual, so there is nothing a decency garment is for; the body is drawn bare, and if a human ever says it is weird, it gets scoped then ("I kinda like letting people confirm"). **A beard pokes out below the onion hat**, and the general rule: for any hat and any thing that would be hard to make work under it, the hat is drawn to end where the thing begins, so the combination is allowed; the engine's "covers" flag only replaces what a hat truly replaces (the hair on top of the head), never to dodge a hard combination.

Michael asked for a design brief to redo the character models with three options per category, the naked goober body first and before anything else: agender in body and desire, gender-tunable by dress, hair and beard; skin tones in a fair variety; options for every slot (the belt today is a band drawn by the torso rule, not a layer); three traditionally male hairs, three traditionally female, ~~three facial hairs of which one is a moustache and one a big bushy beard like the vanilla WoW gnome's~~ (superseded 2026-10-13: The character screen is the identity: facial hair after 1.0; the wizard's beard open, 2026-10-18, The valley first; food heals a little; teachers are safe; the wizard; the dead strike what they break); colour options for clothes, body, and hair, with hair and clothes both given access to all; the outline of the characters kept (oversized heads, little bodies, shoulders that show in the walk so armour can go on them, quiet legs under long tops, more distinct shoes); the onion hat (today's hood: the whole head covered but a slit for the eyes, a nub on top) as a look the world leans into; tunic, belt, breeches, boots as the default with no gloves and no hat; eight directions. **Drafted as `25-character-art-brief.md`.** The answer to "do you do animations or does design": **the rig does**; a drawn part is painted once per facing in the rest pose and the game carries it through every animation, so the artist delivers parts, not frames, and garments are stiff by that pipeline (no cloaks that sway). Colour is data: one drawing per shape, the game dyes. Not started.

### 2026-10-12 — The third-hit rule off for testing, and the finisher cut to 1.5 (Michael)

To test the melee without them: **the third-hit knockdown is switched off** (`combo.enabled: false` in `data/combat.json`; the code and its hit counting are kept, `hits` and `window` still there) and **the downed-enemy bonus is 1.5, not 2** (`finisher.mult`). **Effect, measured:** the sword's two-swing kill (34 + 68) is gone, as the mace's: with a 1.5 finisher a first-hit knockdown gives 34 + 51 for the sword and 38 + 57 for the mace, both short of 100, so a fresh 100-hp dead one now takes 3 swings with either, every time (79 of 80 trials; one outlier from the test's own repositioning). The mace keeps its 60 percent knockdown chance, so a mace still floors them more often, it just does not shorten the kill. The knockdown remains the "crit without being one" for what it does next (the dead one on the floor for 2 s, hit at 1.5x), but it no longer shortens the kill. Both are one line each to put back.

### 2026-10-12 — Dual wielding on the left button, with an exposure price (Michael, question 23)

The right button is the menu, so an off-hand swing there never worked; **ruled: dual wielding is on the left button.** Any one-handed weapon (sword, hatchet, mace, stick, torch) goes in either hand; clicking faster than the main weapon's recovery swings the off hand, so spamming goes back and forth at up to twice the rate with two strong weapons. **The drawback, Michael's: glass cannon.** The first idea was an anti-shield (more damage taken in front, more at the sides, fatal from behind). **Built as state, not loadout (Claude's refinement):** an off-hand swing exposes you for 2.5 s, and exposed you take 1.5x from the front, 2.5x at the sides, a hundred times (fatal) from behind; carrying a torch or two weapons costs nothing, because a loadout penalty would tax the ordinary night kit (a sword and a torch) and the torch only swings at a click rate the sword cannot keep. Open: whether the fatal back should be a big multiple that kills in two bites instead (`behind` in `data/combat.json`). Answers question 23 (option b, with a price).

### 2026-10-12 — Stagger by weight, immunity, and a bite under way is not cancelled (Michael: "the big one", question 29b)

**What it was:** every hit staggered the dead one for 0.3 s and cancelled a bite it had begun, and the push on every hit sent it back a step, so a player who kept hitting held a zombie in a stun-lock and it never got near enough to bite. **What it is now:** (1) **a hit staggers by chance, by weapon weight** (`staggerChance` on the weapon; `stagger.chance` 0.5 is the default): stick and plank 0.3, torch and fists 0.2, hatchet, sword, spear stab 0.5, halberd sweep 0.7, **mace, halberd thrust, spear throw, and the shove always (1)**. (2) **After a stagger ends, the dead one cannot be staggered again for 0.6 s** (`stagger.immunity`), so a stream of hits cannot hold it. (3) ~~**A bite under way is not cancelled by a stagger** unless the weapon `interrupts` (the mace, the thrust, the throw, the shove)~~ (narrowed 2026-10-23, The melee feel pass: the bite gets a wind-up and any landed hit in it cancels the bite; `interrupts` is retired); a knockdown still cancels anything. Damage, the push, and the knockdown chance are unchanged (the third-hit rule is still off; Michael's earlier cuts stand). **Measured** (a scripted player spamming a sword at a charging crowd, aggro on, 5 trials): five zombies from all sides landed **7.8 bites a fight against 6.0 under the old rules** (30 percent more), and a lone zombie against a sword still lands none (it dies on the way in), while a stick lets one bite through half the time; the player in that test died in both, with bleed and infection now live. Not changed, for Michael to judge by play: the push per hit (0.27 tiles for the sword) still sends a zombie back a step; and the spin-click question (a turn-rate cap) is the other half of 29b and is not built.

### 2026-10-12 — Weapon durability: permanent, hits only, evaporates (Michael); and a big pass to come

Michael, thinking about the dual-wield balance: **durability is wanted, and as a one-way thing: a bar that only goes down, wears only on a landed hit (never on a miss: a fairness rule), and when empty the item evaporates; ~~no repair, no mending,~~ no gradual dulling.** (superseded 2026-10-19, A production skill's best is never the best of its kind; repair is a stop-gap: repair at the anvil is a stop-gap, never a reason to stop making new ones) It waits for a crafting and smith loop to feed it (the weapons are consumables, so the smith and crafting stations are their supply). ~~Claude's earlier "repair at a station" idea is withdrawn.~~ (superseded 2026-10-19, A production skill's best is never the best of its kind; repair is a stop-gap: repair at the anvil, as a stop-gap) **Large inventories and the bookkeeping they bring are wanted** ("it's how I would want to play"); Claude is to say when performance becomes a concern (the sprite atlas is the first thing to watch). A crowd breaking weapons is not a worry yet and would be a sign to ease something. He has a **big "what should we add" pass planned**: smith, cooking and crafting stations, a wheelbarrow (an immovable crate), seeds for each crop, many tool models, new interfaces, and a rigorous way to handle item interactions: drafted as a to-do list with questions in `22-the-big-pass.md`.

### 2026-10-12 — Bleed stacks, with Michael's two bounds (question 29b)

**Built (`src/bleed.js`), before the stagger fix, so it can be tested together with the infection marker.** A landed bite that did damage opens a wound with a **50 percent** chance (`bleed.chance`; measured 190 of 400): one more stack, up to **5**, the timer back to **12 seconds**. The wound drains health by stack count, **0.4, 1.6, 3.6, 6.4, 10 a second** for 1 to 5 stacks (`dpsByStacks`: a table that rises faster than the count, because **Michael's bounds** are two: **five fresh stacks kept refreshed, with no other damage, must take 8 to 10 seconds or more to kill from full health** (he counted it out in Mississippis: measured **10.0 s**; four stacks 15.6 s, three 27.8 s), **and a single stack, unless you are already very low, is a nuisance** (**under 5 health in all**: 4.8, and it closes by itself)). Unrefreshed, three stacks cost about 43 and four about 77 before they close, so the wound is the thing to bandage. **A bandage** (new item, stacks to 5, "Apply" in its menu, in the testing chest and `giveKit()`) closes it at once. A moodle ("Bleeding" to "Bleeding out", a drop for now) shows the stack count (narrowed 2026-10-19, Debuffs apart from moodles; only pemmican stacks; hearty food carries buffs; the belt knife is the Dirk: bleeding is now a debuff, apart from the moodles); a message names the first wound. **Not done:** bleed from anything but bites, a bandage from cloth, health potions (Michael: later, needs balancing), the sound and the drips.

### 2026-10-12 — The virus comes back as a marker (Michael, question 29b)

*Narrowed (2026-10-19, Unsafe water: a sickness that may come and passes; the skin sips only clean water; crops take any water): a drink of unsafe water is a second way in, a small chance.*

(the build's record is `docs/history/02-build-log.md`, same heading)

Michael, on the wounds question: the **stack-bleed** is liked, health potions are an idea for later and need balancing around, and **the virus is wanted back because it adds stakes**: infection "alarming but not uncommon", a thing to take care of that **should not turn you inside one to two in-game days**, with ways to mitigate it that feel fair (an idea: mushrooms, some crafting). **First step, built as a mock-up (`src/infection.js`):** a landed bite that did damage (after the guard and any exposure) infects with a **20 percent chance** (`infection.chance` in `data/combat.json`); the infection is a clock of **1.5 in-game days** (`infection.days`, 1080 real seconds at 720 per day) that only shows itself, as a **moodle going green (Bitten), yellow (Fevered), orange (Sick), red (Turning)** at a quarter each, held red after. **It does nothing else: no turning, no death, no damage.** A message says the bite broke the skin.

### 2026-10-12 — Multi-hit is the easy button, off by default (Michael)

After a long play with sword and shield (no trees in the testing mode, so easier than the real map): **holding right to guard and backing away with a horde trailing has recreated Zomboid's walk-backwards play, and Michael likes it a lot**; he never uses multi-hit in Zomboid because it is the easy button, and it feels overpowered here for the same reason, "which tells you we've built a coherent combat system". **Ruled: the Multi-hit setting stays in the menu, 100 percent, and is off by default as the intended way to play** (the default was on; his own pane already had it off). No special rule for the guard: it already cuts what a blow does to 35 percent. **Open for him:** with multi-hit off by default, the halberd's sweep loses its crowd identity and becomes a forgiving-aim first-target blow against the thrust's narrow reach; one answer is to make the halberd's sweep cleave regardless of the setting (the only weapon that does, paid for in its slow swing and 12 stamina); not done.

**The halberd tuned** the same night after "potentially OP": a slower swing and a larger stamina cost on both modes, a slightly shorter reach, a longer drawing (`21`).

### 2026-10-12 — The halberd's thrust and a longer picture (Michael)

Michael loved the second mode and asked for a longer drawing to match the green hit outline. **Built:** a thrust on the middle button or `Q` (reach 3.0, a 12 degree arc, 40 damage, 1.1 s; tuned the same night to reach 2.9, 1.3 s, 15 stamina, `21`; nearest only), and a held picture that can be larger than 32 px with its own grip (the halberd's is 112 px, 52 px of pole forward of the hand). **Any weapon can carry an `alt` block now.** `21` has the numbers.

### 2026-10-12 — The weapon order, and the halberd (Michael)

**Design principle: the mace is the strongest (crush the skull) and the sword second (cut off the head), even if they end up overtuned.** The halberd, roughed out the same day: two-handed, reach 2.3, damage 30 (below the sword), 0.8 s swing, no shield; a second mode (thrust on the middle button) is for later. Numbers and a measured kill time in `21`.

### 2026-10-12 — The knockdown is the game's crit (Michael's finding, `14`)

Michael: a sword knockdown on the first swing makes the follow-up a two-shot, "it feels like a crit without actually being one." **Measured (120 kills of a fresh 100-hp dead one per weapon, real swings, 2026-10-12):** sword 2 swings 32 percent of the time and 3 otherwise (mean 2.68, 1.34 s); mace 2 swings 67 percent (mean 2.33, 1.52 s); hatchet 3 or 4 (mean 3.5, 1.93 s); spear stab 3 or 4 (3.55, 2.13 s); stick 5 or 6 (5.5, 2.75 s). **The rule behind it:** every hit has a knockdown chance (0.3; the mace 0.6); a hit on a downed one is a finisher at twice the damage; so a first-hit knockdown kills in two when `damage + 2 x damage >= 100`, which is `damage >= 34`: the sword (34) and the mace (38) have it, the hatchet (30), spear (30) and stick do not. **It is emergent, not designed:** the sword clears the line by two hit points (34 + 68 = 102 against a 100-hp dead one; at 33 it vanishes), and the dead's hit points moving, a tired swing (half damage), or a guarded one (35 percent) all break it. **Ruled: keep it, and call the knockdown the crit**: the chance is visible (the dead one hits the floor) rather than a hidden roll on damage, which is why it feels fair. What to decide only if the sword proves too strong overall: the sword's knockdown chance (0.3), its damage by one point, or its stamina; and if the dead gain varied hit points, whether the two-shot line should be a stated rule (a `heavy` weapon tier) instead of an accident of 34.

### 2026-10-12 — The guard: total in front, half at the sides, attack from behind it (Michael, `21`)

After "Fixed!!": attacks are allowed from behind the guard at much less damage (35 percent); walking guarded and the free draw both slow to 0.45; **a zombie's bite is one event with fixed damage, so the guard works as a scale of it by the attacker's position against the aim: nothing in front (100 degrees), half to the sides (220 in all), whole from behind**, which Michael hoped was possible and it is; blocks cost 5 stamina and **no stamina regenerates while the guard is up**, so it cannot be lived in (Claude's addition, to keep the fight grindy rather than a stalemate). Numbers in `21`; the first build's "nothing while guarded" rule is struck.

### 2026-10-12 — The hands remember what a two-hander pushed out (a bug of Michael's finding, `21`)

Flipping through weapons with a shield on lost the guard for good: the bow pushed the shield into the bag and nothing put it back. **Ruling (Claude's, small):** an item pushed out of the off hand by a two-handed one is remembered and returns, once, when a one-handed item takes the main hand with the off hand empty, or the two-hander is put away, if it is still in the bag or on the belt. Michael may want it narrower (the shield only) or off; the memory is `player.offMemory` in `inventory.js`.

### 2026-10-12 — The guard shows; the bow's free draw slowed (Michael, `21`)

Options 1 and 2 for the guard's legibility are built (the raised shield, the spark, the flash, the stamina pulse; the raised-guard pose is for the art). **The bow's free draw goes from 0.9 s to 1.4 s, the braced draw stays 0.6 s**, so the two modes are distinct and the bow loses the race to melee (about 4.9 s a kill free, 2.5 braced, the sword 1.5): the safe weapon is the slow one. Michael: "the shooting mechanics are perfect", so only the numbers move; `21` lists the knobs in the order to turn them, including the dead learning where a shot came from.

### 2026-10-12 — Combat difficulty and infection are one dial (Michael, after the first play of sword and spear)

Michael fought a crowd with the new weapons: eight kills, a spear thrown, a panic, a death: "working as intended". The principle he asked to be written down: **with no infection mechanic, combat has to be hard enough on its own to justify the dead being a threat at all** (a bite that costs nothing lasting must still cost something in the moment: positioning, stamina, a thrown spear that leaves the hand empty). **If an infection mechanic is added later (`05`, "Infection, treatable"), combat must be eased to match**, or the two together read as punishment and not as tension. Either lever may move; they must be set together. A crowd that one player on a good run can just barely clear is the target feel for now. **Michael's softening, the same day, on adding the infection marker:** "maybe easing combat to match": ease it only if real consequences do not land somewhere that feels balanced; he does not want to force himself to keep tweaking, and the caveat is that if infection's real effects feel right alongside the combat as it is, nothing is eased.

### 2026-10-12 — The mouse in combat: three roles, the Zomboid split, and the special boy (Michael, question 29)

**Left is the main hand's primary; right is the off hand (hold to block with a shield, the off-hand weapon or torch when dual wielding); middle click, with a key twin, is the main hand's other mode** (the spear's throw; later weapons with two modes, a halberd). Accepted fully, with one caveat kept open: Michael may yet put both blows of two one-handed weapons on the left button if left-and-right feels clunky; Claude's version is built first and play-tested. **Spear and shield is intentional and special**: the only hand pairing with no empty role (stab, block, throw), so it gets drawbacks: the spear may break on a throw (~~about half the time, to tune~~ one in four, hit or miss, since 2026-10-24, The spear breaks one throw in four), and spears are big in the grid, so "one shield and fifty spears" is not a thing; the throw is a last resort with consequences. A spear can be held with a shield, **never with another one-handed weapon**. Spears throw from the middle button and bows loose from the left, and that is fine: they are different weapons that only share an aiming system the player never sees (no guide by default). **The actions menu stays on the right button, Zomboid's way: a tap opens it, a hold blocks.** Kept because the menu is a debug view of everything a thing offers, and because the right button is the only honest home for click-to-move, the left being "swing the held thing" and "poke the interactable". **A held block slows the player by about a fifth to a third and makes them much safer**, in a form to design with the shield. **The bow brace** (Michael, the same hour): hold right with a bow to move slower and draw faster, two draw speeds to master, never one in the middle; a tap of the right while drawn cancels the draw without loosing (he had meant to ask for it; it felt natural). Numbers to start from: braced movement 0.2, ~~draw 0.9 to 0.6 seconds~~ (superseded 2026-10-12: The guard shows; the bow's free draw slowed: 1.4 s free, 0.6 braced). None of this is built yet: the spear's stab, the throw moving to the middle button, the block, the brace, and the split are the next combat work after the fire test below.

Same draw, same ground line and shadow, same landing-only hits and obstacles as the bow (`20`); about 0.30 of the bow's range (6.5 tiles) and ~~3 times an arrow's damage (114)~~, so a full draw kills ~~a 100-hp~~ the dead one (narrowed 2026-10-24, Primary's reading in building `41` step 6, Michael's to overrule: with the dead at 150 the throw is 150, keeping the ruling's point that a full throw kills; the arrow multiple could not stand beside it). **Nothing is left in the hand after a throw**: the spear is the projectile, so you pick it up (E) or equip another; a spear that hits stays in the body and comes out in its loot, like an arrow.
